========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I, uh... well, I guess I'm here because I just don't feel like myself anymore. It's like... I used to do things. I'd go hiking up near the ridge, you know? The trails up there get really muddy in the spring, but the view is worth it. I saw a bald eagle up there once. Just circling. But I stopped going. I stopped coaching, too. And playing guitar. I used to play a lot of James Taylor, but the strings got rusty, and I just... I don't know. People call me, and I don't call them back. My brother called me three times last week about his truck. It's a Ford, keeps stalling at red lights. I told him a year ago it was the alternator, but he never listens. Anyway, I just sit in my rental now. It's a small place. The radiator clanks at night. I sit there for hours, and I know I should be doing things—there's a stack of mail on the counter, and the dishes... but I just can't make myself start. I just sit there.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it's... things have really been unraveling since I moved into this smaller rental. That was right after my divorce, so I guess... about a year and two months ago. I remember because it was right around the time the city changed all the parking meters downtown. They put in those new digital ones where you have to type in your license plate number. People absolutely hate them. I was working security down at the municipal building back then, and folks would always come up to me complaining about the meters, like I could do anything about it. I just wore a badge, you know? I wasn't parking enforcement. 

But my ex-wife kept the house in the settlement. It had this massive oak tree in the front yard that dropped acorns everywhere in the fall. Sounded like hail hitting the aluminum awning over the porch. I spent half my weekends on a ladder cleaning those gutters out. Gutters get clogged so fast if you don't stay on top of them, and then water backs up under the shingles, and you're looking at thousands in roof damage. I actually told Gary—he's the landlord at my new rental—I told him his gutters are sagging on the south side of the building. He just waved me off. He drives this old Buick LeSabre, always smells like stale cigars and peppermint. But yeah... about a year and two months. Ever since the divorce and moving into that place.

Depressed? I don't... I wouldn't say I feel sad. I don't really cry or feel down like that. It's more like just... blank. You know? Tired? No, I wouldn't say I'm tired. It's not really an energy thing. I mean, I'm not physically exhausted or anything, I'm just... still. But my energy is fine. It's not like my battery is drained. 

Speaking of batteries, the backup generators at the municipal building where I used to work security, they run on these massive industrial diesel engines. They test them on the first Tuesday of every month at exactly 10 AM. You can hear this deep vibration humming right through the floorboards. One time during a bad summer storm, the main grid went down because a squirrel chewed through a transformer over on 4th Street. You'd be amazed how many power outages are actually caused by squirrels. They have to chew on things constantly because their teeth never stop growing. Just like any rodent, you know? My uncle Arthur had a pet guinea pig when I was a kid, and he used to give it these little wooden blocks from a craft store to chew on. Guinea pigs make this really weird squeaking noise when they know you're about to feed them. It sounds exactly like a rusty cabinet hinge. He kept it in a glass aquarium, which you're really not supposed to do because of the air circulation. But yeah, no, I don't feel physically tired. I just sit there.

I was working security down at the municipal building back then, and folks would always come up to me complaining about the meters, like I could do anything about it. I just wore a badge, you know? I wasn't parking enforcement. \n\nBut my ex-wife kept the house in the settlement. It had this massive oak tree in the front yard that dropped acorns everywhere in the fall. Sounded like hail hitting the aluminum awning over the porch. I spent half my weekends on a ladder cleaning those gutters out. Gutters get clogged so fast if you don't stay on top of them, and then water backs up under the shingles, and you're looking at thousands in roof damage. I actually told Gary—he's the landlord at my new rental—I told him his gutters are sagging on the south side of the building. He just waved me off. He drives this old Buick LeSabre, always smells like stale cigars and peppermint. But yeah... about a year and two months. Ever since the divorce and moving into that place.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Depressed? I don't... I wouldn't say I feel sad. I don't really cry or feel down like that. It's more like just... blank. You know? Sad is when you actually feel something, like when my buddy's dog got hit by that delivery van. It was a FedEx truck, one of those big white step vans. The driver felt absolutely terrible, he actually sat on the curb and cried. You don't see delivery guys do that much, they're always on such a tight schedule now. They have those GPS trackers in the trucks that monitor if they idle for more than thirty seconds. A guy I used to work security with, Dave, he took a job driving for one of those logistics places and said it's a nightmare. You can't even stop to use the restroom. He told me he keeps an empty Gatorade bottle in the cab. The lemon-lime ones, I think, because they have a wider mouth. But no... I don't feel sad like that driver did. I don't really feel much of anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel tired or have low energy most of the time, even when you’re not doing much?\n\nPatient: Tired? No, I wouldn't say I'm tired. It's not really an energy thing. I mean, I'm not physically exhausted or anything, I'm just... still. But my energy is fine. It's not like my battery is drained. \n\nSpeaking of batteries, the backup generators at the municipal building where I used to work security, they run on these massive industrial diesel engines. They test them on the first Tuesday of every month at exactly 10 AM. You can hear this deep vibration humming right through the floorboards. One time during a bad summer storm, the main grid went down because a squirrel chewed through a transformer over on 4th Street. You'd be amazed how many power outages are actually caused by squirrels. They have to chew on things constantly because their teeth never stop growing. Just like any rodent, you know? My uncle Arthur had a pet guinea pig when I was a kid, and he used to give it these little wooden blocks from a craft store to chew on. Guinea pigs make this really weird squeaking noise when they know you're about to feed them. It sounds exactly like a rusty cabinet hinge. He kept it in a glass aquarium, which you're really not supposed to do because of the air circulation. But yeah, no, I don't feel physically tired. I just sit there.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight over the past year?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 45 / protective_service_worker / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Appetite? No, not really. I don't think my weight has changed either. My belts are all on the same notch. I usually just eat the same things anyway. There's a deli right by my new rental, and I'll just get a turkey sandwich most days. The guy who runs it wraps them in that really thick brown butcher paper. You know the kind with the waxy coating on the inside? It's really good for keeping the bread from drying out. 

Actually, my grandfather used to use paper a lot like that—oiled paper, really—to wrap up his tools in the garage so they wouldn't rust. He lived right on the coast, and the salt air up there just eats through metal. It's unbelievable. You'd leave a bicycle outside for a weekend and the chain would be completely seized up by Monday. WD-40 doesn't even touch it once the salt gets into the links. You actually need a real penetrating oil, like PB Blaster. It comes in this can with a yellow and red label. Smells absolutely terrible, though, kind of like burnt plastic and sulfur. If you get it on your hands, you can't wash the smell off for a week, no matter how much pumice soap you use. You just have to wait for your skin cells to shed. But no... no changes in my eating or my weight, I don't think.

Blame myself? No, I don't... I haven't really done anything to feel guilty about. I don't feel worthless, no. Usually when things go wrong, people are just too quick to point fingers anyway. Like this one guy I worked with on the night shift, Greg. Management tried to blame him when the loading dock door got stuck open overnight, but it wasn't his fault at all. It was the hydraulic arm on the door. 

Those heavy commercial door closers, they have this fluid inside them that regulates the swing speed, you know? If the O-ring seals go bad, the fluid just leaks right out down the frame. It's this really sticky, clear oil. It smells exactly like sewing machine oil, actually. My mom used to have one of those old Singer sewing machines, the vintage kind built right into the wooden table with the heavy cast-iron pedal at the bottom. She used to make these thick curtains on it to keep the drafts out of the house during the winter. Heavy velvet ones. Velvet is absolutely terrible for collecting dust, though. You have to use a special lint brush on it, the kind with those directional red bristles. You can't just vacuum it or the suction completely ruins the pile of the fabric and leaves these permanent streaks. 

But yeah, no... I don't blame myself for things. I'm just... fine.

They test them on the first Tuesday of every month at exactly 10 AM. You can hear this deep vibration humming right through the floorboards. One time during a bad summer storm, the main grid went down because a squirrel chewed through a transformer over on 4th Street. The guy who runs it wraps them in that really thick brown butcher paper. You know the kind with the waxy coating on the inside? It's really good for keeping the bread from drying out. \n\nActually, my grandfather used to use paper a lot like that—oiled paper, really—to wrap up his tools in the garage so they wouldn't rust. WD-40 doesn't even touch it once the salt gets into the links. You actually need a real penetrating oil, like PB Blaster. It comes in this can with a yellow and red label. It was the hydraulic arm on the door. \n\nThose heavy commercial door closers, they have this fluid inside them that regulates the swing speed, you know? My mom used to have one of those old Singer sewing machines, the vintage kind built right into the wooden table with the heavy cast-iron pedal at the bottom. She used to make these thick curtains on it to keep the drafts out of the house during the winter. Heavy velvet ones. Velvet is absolutely terrible for collecting dust, though. You have to use a special lint brush on it, the kind with those directional red bristles. You can't just vacuum it or the suction completely ruins the pile of the fabric and leaves these permanent streaks. \n\nBut yeah, no... I don't blame myself for things. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Moving slowly? Restless? No, I wouldn't say I'm restless. I don't feel like I have to move around or anything. And I don't think I'm moving slowly. I just... sit. 

It's actually kind of nice, just sitting. You notice things more when you're not rushing around. Like the dust motes in the air. Have you ever really watched them? They look like they're just floating randomly, but they're actually caught in these tiny thermal currents in the room. Heat rises from the radiators—well, in the old building I used to work in, they had those massive cast-iron steam radiators. They clanked so loud when the boiler kicked on in the basement. The boiler room was completely off-limits, but I had to do rounds down there twice a night. It smelled like old rust and... wet cement. You know that smell? When it first starts raining on a hot sidewalk in the summer? Petrichor, I think they call it. It's actually caused by a bacteria in the soil that releases a chemical when the water hits it. It's funny how something so small can make a smell that strong. Kind of like how a single drop of food coloring can change a whole glass of water. My sister used to do that trick when we were kids, making green milk for St. Patrick's Day. It always tasted weird to me, even though it was just regular milk. Food coloring has this slightly bitter, metallic taste if you put a drop straight on your tongue. 

But no, I don't feel restless. I just don't really move much.

Ever since the divorce and moving into that place.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Depressed? I don't... I wouldn't say I feel sad. I don't really cry or feel down like that. It's more like just... blank. You know? Sad is when you actually feel something, like when my buddy's dog got hit by that delivery van. It was a FedEx truck, one of those big white step vans. The driver felt absolutely terrible, he actually sat on the curb and cried. You don't see delivery guys do that much, they're always on such a tight schedule now. They have those GPS trackers in the trucks that monitor if they idle for more than thirty seconds. A guy I used to work security with, Dave, he took a job driving for one of those logistics places and said it's a nightmare. You can't even stop to use the restroom. He told me he keeps an empty Gatorade bottle in the cab. The lemon-lime ones, I think, because they have a wider mouth. But no... I don't feel sad like that driver did. I don't really feel much of anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel tired or have low energy most of the time, even when you’re not doing much?\n\nPatient: Tired? No, I wouldn't say I'm tired. It's not really an energy thing. I mean, I'm not physically exhausted or anything, I'm just... still. But my energy is fine. It's not like my battery is drained. \n\nSpeaking of batteries, the backup generators at the municipal building where I used to work security, they run on these massive industrial diesel engines. They test them on the first Tuesday of every month at exactly 10 AM. You can hear this deep vibration humming right through the floorboards. One time during a bad summer storm, the main grid went down because a squirrel chewed through a transformer over on 4th Street. You'd be amazed how many power outages are actually caused by squirrels. They have to chew on things constantly because their teeth never stop growing. Just like any rodent, you know? My uncle Arthur had a pet guinea pig when I was a kid, and he used to give it these little wooden blocks from a craft store to chew on. Guinea pigs make this really weird squeaking noise when they know you're about to feed them. It sounds exactly like a rusty cabinet hinge. He kept it in a glass aquarium, which you're really not supposed to do because of the air circulation. But yeah, no, I don't feel physically tired. I just sit there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight over the past year?\n\nPatient: Appetite? No, not really. I don't think my weight has changed either. My belts are all on the same notch. I usually just eat the same things anyway. There's a deli right by my new rental, and I'll just get a turkey sandwich most days. The guy who runs it wraps them in that really thick brown butcher paper. You know the kind with the waxy coating on the inside? It's really good for keeping the bread from drying out. \n\nActually, my grandfather used to use paper a lot like that—oiled paper, really—to wrap up his tools in the garage so they wouldn't rust. He lived right on the coast, and the salt air up there just eats through metal. It's unbelievable. You'd leave a bicycle outside for a weekend and the chain would be completely seized up by Monday. WD-40 doesn't even touch it once the salt gets into the links. You actually need a real penetrating oil, like PB Blaster. It comes in this can with a yellow and red label. Smells absolutely terrible, though, kind of like burnt plastic and sulfur. If you get it on your hands, you can't wash the smell off for a week, no matter how much pumice soap you use. You just have to wait for your skin cells to shed. But no... no changes in my eating or my weight, I don't think.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're not worth much or have been blaming yourself for things more than usual?\n\nPatient: Blame myself? No, I don't... I haven't really done anything to feel guilty about. I don't feel worthless, no. Usually when things go wrong, people are just too quick to point fingers anyway. Like this one guy I worked with on the night shift, Greg. Management tried to blame him when the loading dock door got stuck open overnight, but it wasn't his fault at all. It was the hydraulic arm on the door. \n\nThose heavy commercial door closers, they have this fluid inside them that regulates the swing speed, you know? If the O-ring seals go bad, the fluid just leaks right out down the frame. It's this really sticky, clear oil. It smells exactly like sewing machine oil, actually. My mom used to have one of those old Singer sewing machines, the vintage kind built right into the wooden table with the heavy cast-iron pedal at the bottom. She used to make these thick curtains on it to keep the drafts out of the house during the winter. Heavy velvet ones. Velvet is absolutely terrible for collecting dust, though. You have to use a special lint brush on it, the kind with those directional red bristles. You can't just vacuum it or the suction completely ruins the pile of the fabric and leaves these permanent streaks. \n\nBut yeah, no... I don't blame myself for things. I'm just... fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve been moving more slowly than usual, or have trouble sitting still, like you feel restless inside?\n\nPatient: Moving slowly? Restless? No, I wouldn't say I'm restless. I don't feel like I have to move around or anything. And I don't think I'm moving slowly. I just... sit. \n\nIt's actually kind of nice, just sitting. You notice things more when you're not rushing around. Like the dust motes in the air. Have you ever really watched them? They look like they're just floating randomly, but they're actually caught in these tiny thermal currents in the room. Heat rises from the radiators—well, in the old building I used to work in, they had those massive cast-iron steam radiators. They clanked so loud when the boiler kicked on in the basement. The boiler room was completely off-limits, but I had to do rounds down there twice a night. It smelled like old rust and... wet cement. You know that smell? When it first starts raining on a hot sidewalk in the summer? Petrichor, I think they call it. It's actually caused by a bacteria in the soil that releases a chemical when the water hits it. It's funny how something so small can make a smell that strong. Kind of like how a single drop of food coloring can change a whole glass of water. My sister used to do that trick when we were kids, making green milk for St. Patrick's Day. It always tasted weird to me, even though it was just regular milk. Food coloring has this slightly bitter, metallic taste if you put a drop straight on your tongue. \n\nBut no, I don't feel restless. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Guitar? Yeah... I stopped playing. Honestly, I mostly just don't feel like myself anymore. I haven't picked it up in a long time. The strings are probably all oxidized by now. You know, if you don't wipe down the fretboard after you play, the oils and sweat from your fingers just eat right through the bronze winding on acoustic strings. They get these dark, tarnished spots where you press down the most, especially around the second and third frets if you play a lot of open chords. It makes the tone go completely dead, just this dull thud instead of a nice ring. Kind of like when you hit a piece of waterlogged wood. 

Wood rot is a huge problem in those old trail shelters up where I used to hike. I stopped hiking, too, actually. And coaching. I don't even call people back anymore. Some days I'll just sit in that small rental of mine for hours and hours. I know there are things to do, but I just can't make myself start. It's like... it's like a bad starter motor in a patrol vehicle. They have this little gear called a Bendix drive that pops out to engage the flywheel, and if it gets jammed with grit, it just whines and whines and the engine never turns over. You actually have to get under the chassis and hit the casing with a mallet just to unstick it. The mallets with the hard rubber heads work best so you don't crack the aluminum housing. If you use a steel hammer, you'll shatter it right there on the pavement. 

But yeah... I don't really do any of those things I used to enjoy. I just sit there.

I wasn't parking enforcement. \n\nBut my ex-wife kept the house in the settlement. It had this massive oak tree in the front yard that dropped acorns everywhere in the fall. Sounded like hail hitting the aluminum awning over the porch. I spent half my weekends on a ladder cleaning those gutters out. I actually told Gary—he's the landlord at my new rental—I told him his gutters are sagging on the south side of the building. He just waved me off. One time during a bad summer storm, the main grid went down because a squirrel chewed through a transformer over on 4th Street. You'd be amazed how many power outages are actually caused by squirrels. They have to chew on things constantly because their teeth never stop growing. Just like any rodent, you know? My uncle Arthur had a pet guinea pig when I was a kid, and he used to give it these little wooden blocks from a craft store to chew on. Guinea pigs make this really weird squeaking noise when they know you're about to feed them. It sounds exactly like a rusty cabinet hinge. He kept it in a glass aquarium, which you're really not supposed to do because of the air circulation. But yeah, no, I don't feel physically tired. I just sit there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight over the past year?\n\nPatient: Appetite? No, not really. I don't think my weight has changed either. My belts are all on the same notch. I usually just eat the same things anyway. There's a deli right by my new rental, and I'll just get a turkey sandwich most days. The guy who runs it wraps them in that really thick brown butcher paper. You know the kind with the waxy coating on the inside? It's really good for keeping the bread from drying out. \n\nActually, my grandfather used to use paper a lot like that—oiled paper, really—to wrap up his tools in the garage so they wouldn't rust. He lived right on the coast, and the salt air up there just eats through metal. It's unbelievable. You'd leave a bicycle outside for a weekend and the chain would be completely seized up by Monday. WD-40 doesn't even touch it once the salt gets into the links. You actually need a real penetrating oil, like PB Blaster. It comes in this can with a yellow and red label. Smells absolutely terrible, though, kind of like burnt plastic and sulfur. If you get it on your hands, you can't wash the smell off for a week, no matter how much pumice soap you use. You just have to wait for your skin cells to shed. But no... no changes in my eating or my weight, I don't think.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're not worth much or have been blaming yourself for things more than usual?\n\nPatient: Blame myself? No, I don't... I haven't really done anything to feel guilty about. I don't feel worthless, no. Usually when things go wrong, people are just too quick to point fingers anyway. Like this one guy I worked with on the night shift, Greg. Management tried to blame him when the loading dock door got stuck open overnight, but it wasn't his fault at all. It was the hydraulic arm on the door. \n\nThose heavy commercial door closers, they have this fluid inside them that regulates the swing speed, you know? If the O-ring seals go bad, the fluid just leaks right out down the frame. It's this really sticky, clear oil. It smells exactly like sewing machine oil, actually. My mom used to have one of those old Singer sewing machines, the vintage kind built right into the wooden table with the heavy cast-iron pedal at the bottom. She used to make these thick curtains on it to keep the drafts out of the house during the winter. Heavy velvet ones. Velvet is absolutely terrible for collecting dust, though. You have to use a special lint brush on it, the kind with those directional red bristles. You can't just vacuum it or the suction completely ruins the pile of the fabric and leaves these permanent streaks. \n\nBut yeah, no... I don't blame myself for things. I'm just... fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve been moving more slowly than usual, or have trouble sitting still, like you feel restless inside?\n\nPatient: Moving slowly? Restless? No, I wouldn't say I'm restless. I don't feel like I have to move around or anything. And I don't think I'm moving slowly. I just... sit. \n\nIt's actually kind of nice, just sitting. You notice things more when you're not rushing around. Like the dust motes in the air. Have you ever really watched them? They look like they're just floating randomly, but they're actually caught in these tiny thermal currents in the room. Heat rises from the radiators—well, in the old building I used to work in, they had those massive cast-iron steam radiators. They clanked so loud when the boiler kicked on in the basement. The boiler room was completely off-limits, but I had to do rounds down there twice a night. It smelled like old rust and... wet cement. You know that smell? When it first starts raining on a hot sidewalk in the summer? Petrichor, I think they call it. It's actually caused by a bacteria in the soil that releases a chemical when the water hits it. It's funny how something so small can make a smell that strong. Kind of like how a single drop of food coloring can change a whole glass of water. My sister used to do that trick when we were kids, making green milk for St. Patrick's Day. It always tasted weird to me, even though it was just regular milk. Food coloring has this slightly bitter, metallic taste if you put a drop straight on your tongue. \n\nBut no, I don't feel restless. I just don't really move much.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you no longer take pleasure in things you used to enjoy, like playing guitar or hiking?\n\nPatient: Guitar? Yeah... I stopped playing. Honestly, I mostly just don't feel like myself anymore. I haven't picked it up in a long time. The strings are probably all oxidized by now. You know, if you don't wipe down the fretboard after you play, the oils and sweat from your fingers just eat right through the bronze winding on acoustic strings. They get these dark, tarnished spots where you press down the most, especially around the second and third frets if you play a lot of open chords. It makes the tone go completely dead, just this dull thud instead of a nice ring. Kind of like when you hit a piece of waterlogged wood. \n\nWood rot is a huge problem in those old trail shelters up where I used to hike. I stopped hiking, too, actually. And coaching. I don't even call people back anymore. Some days I'll just sit in that small rental of mine for hours and hours. I know there are things to do, but I just can't make myself start. It's like... it's like a bad starter motor in a patrol vehicle. They have this little gear called a Bendix drive that pops out to engage the flywheel, and if it gets jammed with grit, it just whines and whines and the engine never turns over. You actually have to get under the chassis and hit the casing with a mallet just to unstick it. The mallets with the hard rubber heads work best so you don't crack the aluminum housing. If you use a steel hammer, you'll shatter it right there on the pavement. \n\nBut yeah... I don't really do any of those things I used to enjoy. I just sit there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I'm just... fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve been moving more slowly than usual, or have trouble sitting still, like you feel restless inside?\n\nPatient: Moving slowly? Restless? No, I wouldn't say I'm restless. I don't feel like I have to move around or anything. And I don't think I'm moving slowly. I just... sit. \n\nIt's actually kind of nice, just sitting. You notice things more when you're not rushing around. Like the dust motes in the air. Have you ever really watched them? They look like they're just floating randomly, but they're actually caught in these tiny thermal currents in the room. Heat rises from the radiators—well, in the old building I used to work in, they had those massive cast-iron steam radiators. They clanked so loud when the boiler kicked on in the basement. The boiler room was completely off-limits, but I had to do rounds down there twice a night. It smelled like old rust and... wet cement. You know that smell? When it first starts raining on a hot sidewalk in the summer? Petrichor, I think they call it. It's actually caused by a bacteria in the soil that releases a chemical when the water hits it. It's funny how something so small can make a smell that strong. Kind of like how a single drop of food coloring can change a whole glass of water. My sister used to do that trick when we were kids, making green milk for St. Patrick's Day. It always tasted weird to me, even though it was just regular milk. Food coloring has this slightly bitter, metallic taste if you put a drop straight on your tongue. \n\nBut no, I don't feel restless. I just don't really move much.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you no longer take pleasure in things you used to enjoy, like playing guitar or hiking?\n\nPatient: Guitar? Yeah... I stopped playing. Honestly, I mostly just don't feel like myself anymore. I haven't picked it up in a long time. The strings are probably all oxidized by now. You know, if you don't wipe down the fretboard after you play, the oils and sweat from your fingers just eat right through the bronze winding on acoustic strings. They get these dark, tarnished spots where you press down the most, especially around the second and third frets if you play a lot of open chords. It makes the tone go completely dead, just this dull thud instead of a nice ring. Kind of like when you hit a piece of waterlogged wood. \n\nWood rot is a huge problem in those old trail shelters up where I used to hike. I stopped hiking, too, actually. And coaching. I don't even call people back anymore. Some days I'll just sit in that small rental of mine for hours and hours. I know there are things to do, but I just can't make myself start. It's like... it's like a bad starter motor in a patrol vehicle. They have this little gear called a Bendix drive that pops out to engage the flywheel, and if it gets jammed with grit, it just whines and whines and the engine never turns over. You actually have to get under the chassis and hit the casing with a mallet just to unstick it. The mallets with the hard rubber heads work best so you don't crack the aluminum housing. If you use a steel hammer, you'll shatter it right there on the pavement. \n\nBut yeah... I don't really do any of those things I used to enjoy. I just sit there.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever felt like life isn't worth living, or have you had thoughts about not wanting to live anymore?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 45 / protective_service_worker / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Life not worth living? No, I don't... I haven't had thoughts like that. I don't want to die or anything. I just feel kind of blank most of the time. 

Blank is probably the best word for it. It's like a blank sheet of paper, you know? The really heavy stock paper they use for official reports. We had to use this specific 24-pound bond paper for all the incident logs at the precinct. It had this very subtle watermark right in the center if you held it up to the fluorescent lights. The lights in that squad room were awful, though. They had those old magnetic ballasts that hummed constantly, this low 60-hertz buzz that drove you crazy if you were the only one in the room. And they flickered. The tubes were always going bad at the ends, turning that dark, smoky gray color before they finally burned out completely. When you have to replace them, you have to be really careful not to drop them because they have mercury vapor inside. If one breaks, it sounds like a tiny explosion, just this sharp pop, and there's white powder everywhere. My dad actually cut his hand pretty badly on a broken fluorescent tube in his garage once. He had to go get stitches at this urgent care clinic that was right next to a 24-hour diner. The diner had the best cherry pie. Real tart cherries, not that super sweet canned filling that tastes like cough syrup.

But no, I don't think about not wanting to live. I'm just here.

They put in those new digital ones where you have to type in your license plate number. People absolutely hate them. I was working security down at the municipal building back then, and folks would always come up to me complaining about the meters, like I could do anything about it. I just wore a badge, you know? I wasn't parking enforcement. \n\nBut my ex-wife kept the house in the settlement. It had this massive oak tree in the front yard that dropped acorns everywhere in the fall. Sounded like hail hitting the aluminum awning over the porch. I spent half my weekends on a ladder cleaning those gutters out. They test them on the first Tuesday of every month at exactly 10 AM. You can hear this deep vibration humming right through the floorboards. One time during a bad summer storm, the main grid went down because a squirrel chewed through a transformer over on 4th Street. You'd be amazed how many power outages are actually caused by squirrels. They have to chew on things constantly because their teeth never stop growing. Just like any rodent, you know? My uncle Arthur had a pet guinea pig when I was a kid, and he used to give it these little wooden blocks from a craft store to chew on. Guinea pigs make this really weird squeaking noise when they know you're about to feed them. It sounds exactly like a rusty cabinet hinge. He kept it in a glass aquarium, which you're really not supposed to do because of the air circulation. But yeah, no, I don't feel physically tired. I just sit there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight over the past year?\n\nPatient: Appetite? No, not really. I don't think my weight has changed either. My belts are all on the same notch. I usually just eat the same things anyway. There's a deli right by my new rental, and I'll just get a turkey sandwich most days. The guy who runs it wraps them in that really thick brown butcher paper. You know the kind with the waxy coating on the inside? It's really good for keeping the bread from drying out. \n\nActually, my grandfather used to use paper a lot like that—oiled paper, really—to wrap up his tools in the garage so they wouldn't rust. He lived right on the coast, and the salt air up there just eats through metal. It's unbelievable. You'd leave a bicycle outside for a weekend and the chain would be completely seized up by Monday. WD-40 doesn't even touch it once the salt gets into the links. You actually need a real penetrating oil, like PB Blaster. It comes in this can with a yellow and red label. Smells absolutely terrible, though, kind of like burnt plastic and sulfur. If you get it on your hands, you can't wash the smell off for a week, no matter how much pumice soap you use. You just have to wait for your skin cells to shed. But no... no changes in my eating or my weight, I don't think.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're not worth much or have been blaming yourself for things more than usual?\n\nPatient: Blame myself? It was the hydraulic arm on the door. \n\nThose heavy commercial door closers, they have this fluid inside them that regulates the swing speed, you know? If the O-ring seals go bad, the fluid just leaks right out down the frame. It's this really sticky, clear oil. It smells exactly like sewing machine oil, actually. The boiler room was completely off-limits, but I had to do rounds down there twice a night. It smelled like old rust and... wet cement. You know that smell? When it first starts raining on a hot sidewalk in the summer? Petrichor, I think they call it. It's actually caused by a bacteria in the soil that releases a chemical when the water hits it. It's funny how something so small can make a smell that strong. Kind of like how a single drop of food coloring can change a whole glass of water. My sister used to do that trick when we were kids, making green milk for St. Patrick's Day. It always tasted weird to me, even though it was just regular milk. Food coloring has this slightly bitter, metallic taste if you put a drop straight on your tongue. \n\nBut no, I don't feel restless. I just don't really move much.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you no longer take pleasure in things you used to enjoy, like playing guitar or hiking?\n\nPatient: Guitar? Yeah... I stopped playing. Honestly, I mostly just don't feel like myself anymore. I haven't picked it up in a long time. The strings are probably all oxidized by now. You know, if you don't wipe down the fretboard after you play, the oils and sweat from your fingers just eat right through the bronze winding on acoustic strings. They get these dark, tarnished spots where you press down the most, especially around the second and third frets if you play a lot of open chords. It makes the tone go completely dead, just this dull thud instead of a nice ring. I don't even call people back anymore. Some days I'll just sit in that small rental of mine for hours and hours. I know there are things to do, but I just can't make myself start. It's like... it's like a bad starter motor in a patrol vehicle. They have this little gear called a Bendix drive that pops out to engage the flywheel, and if it gets jammed with grit, it just whines and whines and the engine never turns over. You actually have to get under the chassis and hit the casing with a mallet just to unstick it. The mallets with the hard rubber heads work best so you don't crack the aluminum housing. If you use a steel hammer, you'll shatter it right there on the pavement. \n\nBut yeah... I don't really do any of those things I used to enjoy. I just sit there.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like life isn't worth living, or have you had thoughts about not wanting to live anymore?\n\nPatient: Life not worth living? No, I don't... Everything? Yeah, pretty much everything. Like I said before, I stopped hiking, coaching... playing guitar. I don't call people back. Mostly I just don't feel like myself anymore. I don't really have any interest in doing anything. Some days I'll just sit in that small rental of mine for hours and hours. I know there are things to do, but I just can't make myself start. 

It's just this heavy feeling, like wearing a lead apron. They make you wear those at the dentist when they take X-rays, to protect your organs from the radiation. The lead is so dense it blocks the X-ray photons from passing through. Lead is actually a really fascinating metal. It's so soft you can scratch it with your fingernail, but it's incredibly heavy. They used to use it in paint to make it dry faster and resist moisture, which is why old houses are full of it. If the paint starts peeling and chipping, it turns into this sweet-tasting dust. That's why kids used to eat it. It's crazy to think they used to put it in gasoline, too, to stop engine knocking. The exhaust fumes would just coat everything near the highways in this invisible layer of lead dust. My grandfather lived right off Interstate 95, and he was always complaining about the soot on his windowsills. He'd wipe them down with a damp rag every Sunday morning before church. He was very particular about keeping things clean. 

But yeah... I don't really do anything anymore. I just sit there.

I stopped coaching, too. And playing guitar. I used to play a lot of James Taylor, but the strings got rusty, and I just... I don't know. People call me, and I don't call them back. My brother called me three times last week about his truck. It's a Ford, keeps stalling at red lights. I told him a year ago it was the alternator, but he never listens. Anyway, I just sit in my rental now. It's a small place. The radiator clanks at night. I sit there for hours, and I know I should be doing things—there's a stack of mail on the counter, and the dishes... but I just can't make myself start. I just sit there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don't enjoy things as much as you used to, or that you've lost interest in activities you used to like?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess that's exactly it. I just... I don't enjoy them. Like I was saying, I don't feel like myself. I used to love playing that guitar. It's a Martin, acoustic, really nice resonance. I bought it second-hand from a guy who used to play in a bluegrass band out in Kentucky. He had all these stories about playing at county fairs, eating fried dough, you know? But yeah, I stopped playing. Stopped hiking. Stopped coaching the kids. I don't call anyone back. I just sit in the rental for hours. I know I should get up and do the dishes or open the mail, but I just... I can't make myself start. It's like the starter motor in my brother's truck, just clicking and not turning over. I told him he needs to check the battery terminals, they get corroded, especially with the salt on the roads in winter. But yeah, I just sit there.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... things have really been unraveling since I moved into this smaller rental. That was right after my divorce, so I guess... about a year and two months ago. I remember because it was right around the time the city changed all the parking meters downtown. They put in those new digital ones where you have to type in your license plate number. People absolutely hate them. I was working security down at the municipal building back then, and folks would always come up to me complaining about the meters, like I could do anything about it. I just wore a badge, you know? I wasn't parking enforcement. \n\nBut my ex-wife kept the house in the settlement. It had this massive oak tree in the front yard that dropped acorns everywhere in the fall. Sounded like hail hitting the aluminum awning over the porch. I spent half my weekends on a ladder cleaning those gutters out. Gutters get clogged so fast if you don't stay on top of them, and then water backs up under the shingles, and you're looking at thousands in roof damage. I actually told Gary—he's the landlord at my new rental—I told him his gutters are sagging on the south side of the building. He just waved me off. He drives this old Buick LeSabre, always smells like stale cigars and peppermint. But yeah... about a year and two months. Ever since the divorce and moving into that place.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Depressed? I don't... I wouldn't say I feel sad. I don't really cry or feel down like that. It's more like just... blank. You know? Sad is when you actually feel something, like when my buddy's dog got hit by that delivery van. It was a FedEx truck, one of those big white step vans. The driver felt absolutely terrible, he actually sat on the curb and cried. You don't see delivery guys do that much, they're always on such a tight schedule now. They have those GPS trackers in the trucks that monitor if they idle for more than thirty seconds. A guy I used to work security with, Dave, he took a job driving for one of those logistics places and said it's a nightmare. You can't even stop to use the restroom. He told me he keeps an empty Gatorade bottle in the cab. The lemon-lime ones, I think, because they have a wider mouth. But no... I don't feel sad like that driver did. I don't really feel much of anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel tired or have low energy most of the time, even when you’re not doing much?\n\nPatient: Tired? No, I wouldn't say I'm tired. It's not really an energy thing. I mean, I'm not physically exhausted or anything, I'm just... still. But my energy is fine. It's not like my battery is drained. \n\nSpeaking of batteries, the backup generators at the municipal building where I used to work security, they run on these massive industrial diesel engines. They test them on the first Tuesday of every month at exactly 10 AM. You can hear this deep vibration humming right through the floorboards. One time during a bad summer storm, the main grid went down because a squirrel chewed through a transformer over on 4th Street. You'd be amazed how many power outages are actually caused by squirrels. They have to chew on things constantly because their teeth never stop growing. Just like any rodent, you know? My uncle Arthur had a pet guinea pig when I was a kid, and he used to give it these little wooden blocks from a craft store to chew on. Guinea pigs make this really weird squeaking noise when they know you're about to feed them. It sounds exactly like a rusty cabinet hinge. He kept it in a glass aquarium, which you're really not supposed to do because of the air circulation. But yeah, no, I don't feel physically tired. I just sit there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight over the past year?\n\nPatient: Appetite? No, not really. I don't think my weight has changed either. My belts are all on the same notch. I usually just eat the same things anyway. There's a deli right by my new rental, and I'll just get a turkey sandwich most days. The guy who runs it wraps them in that really thick brown butcher paper. You know the kind with the waxy coating on the inside? It's really good for keeping the bread from drying out. \n\nActually, my grandfather used to use paper a lot like that—oiled paper, really—to wrap up his tools in the garage so they wouldn't rust. He lived right on the coast, and the salt air up there just eats through metal. It's unbelievable. You'd leave a bicycle outside for a weekend and the chain would be completely seized up by Monday. WD-40 doesn't even touch it once the salt gets into the links. You actually need a real penetrating oil, like PB Blaster. It comes in this can with a yellow and red label. Smells absolutely terrible, though, kind of like burnt plastic and sulfur. If you get it on your hands, you can't wash the smell off for a week, no matter how much pumice soap you use. You just have to wait for your skin cells to shed. But no... no changes in my eating or my weight, I don't think.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're not worth much or have been blaming yourself for things more than usual?\n\nPatient: Blame myself? No, I don't... I haven't really done anything to feel guilty about. I don't feel worthless, no. Usually when things go wrong, people are just too quick to point fingers anyway. Like this one guy I worked with on the night shift, Greg. Management tried to blame him when the loading dock door got stuck open overnight, but it wasn't his fault at all. It was the hydraulic arm on the door. \n\nThose heavy commercial door closers, they have this fluid inside them that regulates the swing speed, you know? If the O-ring seals go bad, the fluid just leaks right out down the frame. It's this really sticky, clear oil. It smells exactly like sewing machine oil, actually. My mom used to have one of those old Singer sewing machines, the vintage kind built right into the wooden table with the heavy cast-iron pedal at the bottom. She used to make these thick curtains on it to keep the drafts out of the house during the winter. Heavy velvet ones. Velvet is absolutely terrible for collecting dust, though. You have to use a special lint brush on it, the kind with those directional red bristles. You can't just vacuum it or the suction completely ruins the pile of the fabric and leaves these permanent streaks. \n\nBut yeah, no... I don't blame myself for things. I'm just... fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve been moving more slowly than usual, or have trouble sitting still, like you feel restless inside?\n\nPatient: Moving slowly? Restless? No, I wouldn't say I'm restless. I don't feel like I have to move around or anything. And I don't think I'm moving slowly. I just... sit. \n\nIt's actually kind of nice, just sitting. You notice things more when you're not rushing around. Like the dust motes in the air. Have you ever really watched them? They look like they're just floating randomly, but they're actually caught in these tiny thermal currents in the room. Heat rises from the radiators—well, in the old building I used to work in, they had those massive cast-iron steam radiators. They clanked so loud when the boiler kicked on in the basement. The boiler room was completely off-limits, but I had to do rounds down there twice a night. It smelled like old rust and... wet cement. You know that smell? When it first starts raining on a hot sidewalk in the summer? Petrichor, I think they call it. It's actually caused by a bacteria in the soil that releases a chemical when the water hits it. It's funny how something so small can make a smell that strong. Kind of like how a single drop of food coloring can change a whole glass of water. My sister used to do that trick when we were kids, making green milk for St. Patrick's Day. It always tasted weird to me, even though it was just regular milk. Food coloring has this slightly bitter, metallic taste if you put a drop straight on your tongue. \n\nBut no, I don't feel restless. I just don't really move much.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you no longer take pleasure in things you used to enjoy, like playing guitar or hiking?\n\nPatient: Guitar? Yeah... I stopped playing. Honestly, I mostly just don't feel like myself anymore. I haven't picked it up in a long time. The strings are probably all oxidized by now. You know, if you don't wipe down the fretboard after you play, the oils and sweat from your fingers just eat right through the bronze winding on acoustic strings. They get these dark, tarnished spots where you press down the most, especially around the second and third frets if you play a lot of open chords. It makes the tone go completely dead, just this dull thud instead of a nice ring. Kind of like when you hit a piece of waterlogged wood. \n\nWood rot is a huge problem in those old trail shelters up where I used to hike. I stopped hiking, too, actually. And coaching. I don't even call people back anymore. Some days I'll just sit in that small rental of mine for hours and hours. I know there are things to do, but I just can't make myself start. It's like... it's like a bad starter motor in a patrol vehicle. They have this little gear called a Bendix drive that pops out to engage the flywheel, and if it gets jammed with grit, it just whines and whines and the engine never turns over. You actually have to get under the chassis and hit the casing with a mallet just to unstick it. The mallets with the hard rubber heads work best so you don't crack the aluminum housing. If you use a steel hammer, you'll shatter it right there on the pavement. \n\nBut yeah... I don't really do any of those things I used to enjoy. I just sit there.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like life isn't worth living, or have you had thoughts about not wanting to live anymore?\n\nPatient: Life not worth living? No, I don't... I haven't had thoughts like that. I don't want to die or anything. I just feel kind of blank most of the time. \n\nBlank is probably the best word for it. It's like a blank sheet of paper, you know? The really heavy stock paper they use for official reports. We had to use this specific 24-pound bond paper for all the incident logs at the precinct. It had this very subtle watermark right in the center if you held it up to the fluorescent lights. The lights in that squad room were awful, though. They had those old magnetic ballasts that hummed constantly, this low 60-hertz buzz that drove you crazy if you were the only one in the room. And they flickered. The tubes were always going bad at the ends, turning that dark, smoky gray color before they finally burned out completely. When you have to replace them, you have to be really careful not to drop them because they have mercury vapor inside. If one breaks, it sounds like a tiny explosion, just this sharp pop, and there's white powder everywhere. My dad actually cut his hand pretty badly on a broken fluorescent tube in his garage once. He had to go get stitches at this urgent care clinic that was right next to a 24-hour diner. The diner had the best cherry pie. Real tart cherries, not that super sweet canned filling that tastes like cough syrup.\n\nBut no, I don't think about not wanting to live. I'm just here.\n\nDoctor: Have you completely stopped doing all of the activities you used to enjoy, or is there anything at all that still brings you some pleasure or interest?\n\nPatient: Everything? Yeah, pretty much everything. Like I said before, I stopped hiking, coaching... playing guitar. I don't call people back. Mostly I just don't feel like myself anymore. I don't really have any interest in doing anything. Some days I'll just sit in that small rental of mine for hours and hours. I know there are things to do, but I just can't make myself start. \n\nIt's just this heavy feeling, like wearing a lead apron. They make you wear those at the dentist when they take X-rays, to protect your organs from the radiation. The lead is so dense it blocks the X-ray photons from passing through. Lead is actually a really fascinating metal. It's so soft you can scratch it with your fingernail, but it's incredibly heavy. They used to use it in paint to make it dry faster and resist moisture, which is why old houses are full of it. If the paint starts peeling and chipping, it turns into this sweet-tasting dust. That's why kids used to eat it. It's crazy to think they used to put it in gasoline, too, to stop engine knocking. The exhaust fumes would just coat everything near the highways in this invisible layer of lead dust. My grandfather lived right off Interstate 95, and he was always complaining about the soot on his windowsills. He'd wipe them down with a damp rag every Sunday morning before church. He was very particular about keeping things clean. \n\nBut yeah... I don't really do anything anymore. I just sit there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Nothing, really. I think I already mentioned that. I just... I mostly just don't feel like myself anymore, you know? Like I said, I stopped hiking, stopped coaching, the guitar is just sitting there. I don't even call people back. I just don't have it in me. Some days I'll just sit in that small rental for hours and hours. I know there are things to do, I know I should start doing them, but I just can't make myself start. 

Starting things is the hardest part. It's like trying to get a fire going with wet kindling. If the wood is too green or it's been raining, the moisture inside the fibers just turns to steam and puts out whatever little spark you manage to catch. You need dry tinder. Birch bark is actually the best for that. The natural oils in the bark make it highly flammable, even when it's wet on the outside. You can just peel those thin, papery layers right off the tree. You have to be careful not to cut too deep, though, or you'll damage the cambium layer and kill the tree. Trees are surprisingly fragile like that. If you strip a ring of bark all the way around the trunk, it's called girdling, and the tree slowly starves to death because the nutrients can't travel up from the roots anymore. Beavers do that all the time. They just chew right through the bark to get to the sweet sapwood underneath. They have these incredibly strong incisors that never stop growing, so they have to constantly gnaw on wood to file them down. If they didn't, their teeth would eventually grow right through their own skulls. It's a design flaw, really, if you think about it.

But no, I don't enjoy anything. I just sit there in the rental.

My brother called me three times last week about his truck. It's a Ford, keeps stalling at red lights. I told him a year ago it was the alternator, but he never listens. Anyway, I just sit in my rental now. It's a small place. The radiator clanks at night. I sit there for hours, and I know I should be doing things—there's a stack of mail on the counter, and the dishes... but I just can't make myself start. I just sit there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don't enjoy things as much as you used to, or that you've lost interest in activities you used to like?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess that's exactly it. I just... I don't enjoy them. Like I was saying, I don't feel like myself. I used to love playing that guitar. It's a Martin, acoustic, really nice resonance. I bought it second-hand from a guy who used to play in a bluegrass band out in Kentucky. He had all these stories about playing at county fairs, eating fried dough, you know? But yeah, I stopped playing. Stopped hiking. Stopped coaching the kids. I don't call anyone back. I just sit in the rental for hours. I know I should get up and do the dishes or open the mail, but I just... I can't make myself start. It's like the starter motor in my brother's truck, just clicking and not turning over. I told him he needs to check the battery terminals, they get corroded, especially with the salt on the roads in winter. But yeah, I just sit there.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... things have really been unraveling since I moved into this smaller rental. That was right after my divorce, so I guess... about a year and two months ago. I remember because it was right around the time the city changed all the parking meters downtown. They put in those new digital ones where you have to type in your license plate number. People absolutely hate them. I was working security down at the municipal building back then, and folks would always come up to me complaining about the meters, like I could do anything about it. I just wore a badge, you know? I wasn't parking enforcement. \n\nBut my ex-wife kept the house in the settlement. It had this massive oak tree in the front yard that dropped acorns everywhere in the fall. Sounded like hail hitting the aluminum awning over the porch. I spent half my weekends on a ladder cleaning those gutters out. Gutters get clogged so fast if you don't stay on top of them, and then water backs up under the shingles, and you're looking at thousands in roof damage. I actually told Gary—he's the landlord at my new rental—I told him his gutters are sagging on the south side of the building. He just waved me off. He drives this old Buick LeSabre, always smells like stale cigars and peppermint. But yeah... about a year and two months. Ever since the divorce and moving into that place.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Depressed? I don't... I wouldn't say I feel sad. I don't really cry or feel down like that. It's more like just... blank. You know? Sad is when you actually feel something, like when my buddy's dog got hit by that delivery van. It was a FedEx truck, one of those big white step vans. The driver felt absolutely terrible, he actually sat on the curb and cried. You don't see delivery guys do that much, they're always on such a tight schedule now. They have those GPS trackers in the trucks that monitor if they idle for more than thirty seconds. A guy I used to work security with, Dave, he took a job driving for one of those logistics places and said it's a nightmare. You can't even stop to use the restroom. He told me he keeps an empty Gatorade bottle in the cab. The lemon-lime ones, I think, because they have a wider mouth. But no... I don't feel sad like that driver did. I don't really feel much of anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel tired or have low energy most of the time, even when you’re not doing much?\n\nPatient: Tired? No, I wouldn't say I'm tired. It's not really an energy thing. I mean, I'm not physically exhausted or anything, I'm just... still. But my energy is fine. It's not like my battery is drained. \n\nSpeaking of batteries, the backup generators at the municipal building where I used to work security, they run on these massive industrial diesel engines. They test them on the first Tuesday of every month at exactly 10 AM. You can hear this deep vibration humming right through the floorboards. One time during a bad summer storm, the main grid went down because a squirrel chewed through a transformer over on 4th Street. You'd be amazed how many power outages are actually caused by squirrels. They have to chew on things constantly because their teeth never stop growing. Just like any rodent, you know? My uncle Arthur had a pet guinea pig when I was a kid, and he used to give it these little wooden blocks from a craft store to chew on. Guinea pigs make this really weird squeaking noise when they know you're about to feed them. It sounds exactly like a rusty cabinet hinge. He kept it in a glass aquarium, which you're really not supposed to do because of the air circulation. But yeah, no, I don't feel physically tired. I just sit there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight over the past year?\n\nPatient: Appetite? No, not really. I don't think my weight has changed either. My belts are all on the same notch. I usually just eat the same things anyway. There's a deli right by my new rental, and I'll just get a turkey sandwich most days. The guy who runs it wraps them in that really thick brown butcher paper. You know the kind with the waxy coating on the inside? It's really good for keeping the bread from drying out. \n\nActually, my grandfather used to use paper a lot like that—oiled paper, really—to wrap up his tools in the garage so they wouldn't rust. He lived right on the coast, and the salt air up there just eats through metal. It's unbelievable. You'd leave a bicycle outside for a weekend and the chain would be completely seized up by Monday. WD-40 doesn't even touch it once the salt gets into the links. You actually need a real penetrating oil, like PB Blaster. It comes in this can with a yellow and red label. Smells absolutely terrible, though, kind of like burnt plastic and sulfur. If you get it on your hands, you can't wash the smell off for a week, no matter how much pumice soap you use. You just have to wait for your skin cells to shed. But no... no changes in my eating or my weight, I don't think.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're not worth much or have been blaming yourself for things more than usual?\n\nPatient: Blame myself? No, I don't... I haven't really done anything to feel guilty about. I don't feel worthless, no. Usually when things go wrong, people are just too quick to point fingers anyway. Like this one guy I worked with on the night shift, Greg. Management tried to blame him when the loading dock door got stuck open overnight, but it wasn't his fault at all. It was the hydraulic arm on the door. \n\nThose heavy commercial door closers, they have this fluid inside them that regulates the swing speed, you know? If the O-ring seals go bad, the fluid just leaks right out down the frame. It's this really sticky, clear oil. It smells exactly like sewing machine oil, actually. My mom used to have one of those old Singer sewing machines, the vintage kind built right into the wooden table with the heavy cast-iron pedal at the bottom. She used to make these thick curtains on it to keep the drafts out of the house during the winter. Heavy velvet ones. Velvet is absolutely terrible for collecting dust, though. You have to use a special lint brush on it, the kind with those directional red bristles. You can't just vacuum it or the suction completely ruins the pile of the fabric and leaves these permanent streaks. \n\nBut yeah, no... I don't blame myself for things. I'm just... fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve been moving more slowly than usual, or have trouble sitting still, like you feel restless inside?\n\nPatient: Moving slowly? Restless? No, I wouldn't say I'm restless. I don't feel like I have to move around or anything. And I don't think I'm moving slowly. I just... sit. \n\nIt's actually kind of nice, just sitting. You notice things more when you're not rushing around. Like the dust motes in the air. Have you ever really watched them? They look like they're just floating randomly, but they're actually caught in these tiny thermal currents in the room. Heat rises from the radiators—well, in the old building I used to work in, they had those massive cast-iron steam radiators. They clanked so loud when the boiler kicked on in the basement. The boiler room was completely off-limits, but I had to do rounds down there twice a night. It smelled like old rust and... wet cement. You know that smell? When it first starts raining on a hot sidewalk in the summer? Petrichor, I think they call it. It's actually caused by a bacteria in the soil that releases a chemical when the water hits it. It's funny how something so small can make a smell that strong. Kind of like how a single drop of food coloring can change a whole glass of water. My sister used to do that trick when we were kids, making green milk for St. Patrick's Day. It always tasted weird to me, even though it was just regular milk. Food coloring has this slightly bitter, metallic taste if you put a drop straight on your tongue. \n\nBut no, I don't feel restless. I just don't really move much.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you no longer take pleasure in things you used to enjoy, like playing guitar or hiking?\n\nPatient: Guitar? Yeah... I stopped playing. Honestly, I mostly just don't feel like myself anymore. I haven't picked it up in a long time. The strings are probably all oxidized by now. You know, if you don't wipe down the fretboard after you play, the oils and sweat from your fingers just eat right through the bronze winding on acoustic strings. They get these dark, tarnished spots where you press down the most, especially around the second and third frets if you play a lot of open chords. It makes the tone go completely dead, just this dull thud instead of a nice ring. Kind of like when you hit a piece of waterlogged wood. \n\nWood rot is a huge problem in those old trail shelters up where I used to hike. I stopped hiking, too, actually. And coaching. I don't even call people back anymore. Some days I'll just sit in that small rental of mine for hours and hours. I know there are things to do, but I just can't make myself start. It's like... it's like a bad starter motor in a patrol vehicle. They have this little gear called a Bendix drive that pops out to engage the flywheel, and if it gets jammed with grit, it just whines and whines and the engine never turns over. You actually have to get under the chassis and hit the casing with a mallet just to unstick it. The mallets with the hard rubber heads work best so you don't crack the aluminum housing. If you use a steel hammer, you'll shatter it right there on the pavement. \n\nBut yeah... I don't really do any of those things I used to enjoy. I just sit there.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like life isn't worth living, or have you had thoughts about not wanting to live anymore?\n\nPatient: Life not worth living? No, I don't... I haven't had thoughts like that. I don't want to die or anything. I just feel kind of blank most of the time. \n\nBlank is probably the best word for it. It's like a blank sheet of paper, you know? The really heavy stock paper they use for official reports. We had to use this specific 24-pound bond paper for all the incident logs at the precinct. It had this very subtle watermark right in the center if you held it up to the fluorescent lights. The lights in that squad room were awful, though. They had those old magnetic ballasts that hummed constantly, this low 60-hertz buzz that drove you crazy if you were the only one in the room. And they flickered. The tubes were always going bad at the ends, turning that dark, smoky gray color before they finally burned out completely. When you have to replace them, you have to be really careful not to drop them because they have mercury vapor inside. If one breaks, it sounds like a tiny explosion, just this sharp pop, and there's white powder everywhere. My dad actually cut his hand pretty badly on a broken fluorescent tube in his garage once. He had to go get stitches at this urgent care clinic that was right next to a 24-hour diner. The diner had the best cherry pie. Real tart cherries, not that super sweet canned filling that tastes like cough syrup.\n\nBut no, I don't think about not wanting to live. I'm just here.\n\nDoctor: Have you completely stopped doing all of the activities you used to enjoy, or is there anything at all that still brings you some pleasure or interest?\n\nPatient: Everything? Yeah, pretty much everything. Like I said before, I stopped hiking, coaching... playing guitar. I don't call people back. Mostly I just don't feel like myself anymore. I don't really have any interest in doing anything. Some days I'll just sit in that small rental of mine for hours and hours. I know there are things to do, but I just can't make myself start. \n\nIt's just this heavy feeling, like wearing a lead apron. They make you wear those at the dentist when they take X-rays, to protect your organs from the radiation. The lead is so dense it blocks the X-ray photons from passing through. Lead is actually a really fascinating metal. It's so soft you can scratch it with your fingernail, but it's incredibly heavy. They used to use it in paint to make it dry faster and resist moisture, which is why old houses are full of it. If the paint starts peeling and chipping, it turns into this sweet-tasting dust. That's why kids used to eat it. It's crazy to think they used to put it in gasoline, too, to stop engine knocking. The exhaust fumes would just coat everything near the highways in this invisible layer of lead dust. My grandfather lived right off Interstate 95, and he was always complaining about the soot on his windowsills. He'd wipe them down with a damp rag every Sunday morning before church. He was very particular about keeping things clean. \n\nBut yeah... I don't really do anything anymore. I just sit there.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything at all you still enjoy doing, even a little bit, or does nothing give you any pleasure anymore?\n\nPatient: Nothing, really. I think I already mentioned that. I just... I mostly just don't feel like myself anymore, you know? Like I said, I stopped hiking, stopped coaching, the guitar is just sitting there. I don't even call people back. I just don't have it in me. Some days I'll just sit in that small rental for hours and hours. I know there are things to do, I know I should start doing them, but I just can't make myself start. \n\nStarting things is the hardest part. It's like trying to get a fire going with wet kindling. If the wood is too green or it's been raining, the moisture inside the fibers just turns to steam and puts out whatever little spark you manage to catch. You need dry tinder. Birch bark is actually the best for that. The natural oils in the bark make it highly flammable, even when it's wet on the outside. You can just peel those thin, papery layers right off the tree. You have to be careful not to cut too deep, though, or you'll damage the cambium layer and kill the tree. Trees are surprisingly fragile like that. If you strip a ring of bark all the way around the trunk, it's called girdling, and the tree slowly starves to death because the nutrients can't travel up from the roots anymore. Beavers do that all the time. They just chew right through the bark to get to the sweet sapwood underneath. They have these incredibly strong incisors that never stop growing, so they have to constantly gnaw on wood to file them down. If they didn't, their teeth would eventually grow right through their own skulls. It's a design flaw, really, if you think about it.\n\nBut no, I don't enjoy anything. I just sit there in the rental.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
